[10/5/25, 9:21:01 PM] Brevets and TASVB 2027 PBP: ‎Venki Iyengar added Arjun Periasamy and ~ Niranjan
[10/9/25, 7:33:44 PM] Venki Iyengar: Okay, folks
[10/9/25, 7:33:58 PM] Venki Iyengar: Sorry about the delay in talking about all this
[10/9/25, 7:34:03 PM] Venki Iyengar: But here we go ...
[10/9/25, 7:34:21 PM] Venki Iyengar: Welcome to randonneuring .... effectively a sub-sport of cycling
[10/9/25, 7:35:23 PM] Venki Iyengar: I am sure you have all heard us talk enough and more about randonneuring so I will skip explaining what it is. Please ask if you really don't know
[10/9/25, 7:35:48 PM] Venki Iyengar: Any ride in randonneuring is called a brevet
[10/9/25, 7:36:14 PM] Venki Iyengar: A brevet is essentially a self supported, time controlled course provided by a randonneuring club
[10/9/25, 7:36:38 PM] Venki Iyengar: The original randonneuring club is the ACP in France - Audax Club Parisienne.
[10/9/25, 7:37:01 PM] Venki Iyengar: Other randonneuring clubs have to get the courses they make approved by ACP before running them
[10/9/25, 7:37:24 PM] Venki Iyengar: We have 3 randonneuring clubs near us - SFR, SCR and Davis
[10/9/25, 7:38:05 PM] Venki Iyengar: To register for any brevet, you have to first register for RUSA - Randonneurs USA, and then for one of the local clubs.
[10/9/25, 7:38:54 PM] Venki Iyengar: All of us who have been randonneuring all were members of SFR, and I would strongly suggest that to you as well.
[10/9/25, 7:39:21 PM] Venki Iyengar: Each club has an administrator, and SFR RBA is Rob Hawks. Amazing person
[10/9/25, 7:39:47 PM] Venki Iyengar: I will tell you how to do the above in a minute ‎<This message was edited>
[10/9/25, 7:39:59 PM] Venki Iyengar: Now, coming to brevets
[10/9/25, 7:40:21 PM] Venki Iyengar: Brevets are counted in kms since all this originated in Europe
[10/9/25, 7:40:32 PM] Venki Iyengar: The smallest brevet is 200km
[10/9/25, 7:41:11 PM] Venki Iyengar: For whatever reason, there are only some fixed distances in brevets, and these are 200, 300, 400, 600, 1000, 1200
[10/9/25, 7:41:16 PM] Venki Iyengar: LEL is 1500 ish
[10/9/25, 7:41:30 PM] Venki Iyengar: but anything 1200 and above is called a Grand Randonnee
[10/9/25, 7:42:23 PM] Venki Iyengar: The equivalent mile distances are 125, 187.5, 250, 375, 625, 750 ...
[10/9/25, 7:42:54 PM] Venki Iyengar: For now, a number of us are planning to do the DPC 200k on Nov 1st
[10/9/25, 7:43:04 PM] Venki Iyengar: This is one of the easiest 200ks you will find
[10/9/25, 7:43:14 PM] Venki Iyengar: Most brevets have about 50 ft / mile elevation
[10/9/25, 7:43:54 PM] Venki Iyengar: That is the typical elevation at PBP and maybe that is why
[10/9/25, 7:44:34 PM] Venki Iyengar: Thus 200s typically have about 7K elev, though there are some with up to 9K, 300s, typically 9k, 400 around 12k etc.
[10/9/25, 7:44:55 PM] Venki Iyengar: Basically 30 ish ft/km
[10/9/25, 7:45:11 PM] Venki Iyengar: DPC is only about 5.5 K I believe
[10/9/25, 7:45:19 PM] Venki Iyengar: But we will talk more about DPC the week of
[10/9/25, 7:45:52 PM] Venki Iyengar: We have also have a spreadsheet with stops and break times and a whole lot more listed. Of course just suggested, everyone is free to do their own thing
[10/9/25, 7:46:37 PM] Venki Iyengar: Like @⁨Nikhil Mahajan⁩ will go twice as fast as the sheet suggests, @⁨Mihir Sambhus⁩ will cut the break times to 1/4th and Vijayshree will not stop at all etc.
[10/9/25, 7:47:02 PM] Venki Iyengar: Hope all that has made sense so far
[10/9/25, 7:47:12 PM] Venki Iyengar: Back to the start, what do you need to do?
[10/9/25, 7:47:28 PM] Venki Iyengar: a) Register with RUSA
b) Register with SFR
c) Register for the brevet
[10/9/25, 7:47:45 PM] Venki Iyengar: I will put this with links in the description of this group
[10/9/25, 7:47:57 PM] Venki Iyengar: Suggest doing it soon, since it might take a week for the memberships to go through
[10/9/25, 7:48:23 PM] Venki Iyengar: Please remember that everyone running any Randonneuring club or any part of it is a volunteer
[10/9/25, 7:48:37 PM] Venki Iyengar: Registration fees are ridiculously low
[10/9/25, 7:49:01 PM] Venki Iyengar: For any brevet, you have to register latest by the previous Wednesday
[10/9/25, 7:49:14 PM] Venki Iyengar: So that the RBA has time to make lists of all participants et.
[10/9/25, 7:49:46 PM] Venki Iyengar: After you finish a brevet - and this is important - you MUST check in with the finish line folks so that they know you are safe
[10/9/25, 7:50:11 PM] Venki Iyengar: If you decide to abort at any point, there will be a point of contact called DORC (Day of ride contact), who you have to inform
[10/9/25, 7:50:25 PM] Venki Iyengar: But all those details you will get when you register for your first brevet
[10/9/25, 7:50:36 PM] Venki Iyengar: I will talk more about all that on the week of the DPC
[10/9/25, 7:50:45 PM] Venki Iyengar: We will also have a call and go over the sheet and the stops etc.
[10/9/25, 7:51:06 PM] Venki Iyengar: Okay, I think I covered everything I wanted to
[10/9/25, 7:51:07 PM] Venki Iyengar: Any questions?

[10/9/25, 8:14:29 PM] Mihir Sambhus: One other thing I would add 
Randonneuring is basically a sport needing endurance and self sufficiency. You don’t need to go super fast but you need to go steady and long. And take care of all the bike repairs or food and rests on your own. The self sufficiency is big learning experience and you learn as you do longer rides.
[10/9/25, 8:36:59 PM] Shriram: and to add it’s a fantastic opportunity to meet and ride some really unique characters that you could get the opportunity to meet. and those long hours of conversations, silences, digging deep and finishing together with your fellow riders forges friendships that last! ‎<This message was edited>

[10/18/25, 5:16:54 PM] Arjun Periasamy: What do you usually eat on long rides?
[10/18/25, 5:18:13 PM] Arjun Periasamy: Anything that’s not gels becomes a hassle to carry/store and is harder to eat while biking. I’ve tried chapathi wraps/rice but they’re bulky.
[10/18/25, 5:19:23 PM] Venki Iyengar: I think this is a classic difference in thinking between tri folks and randonneurs ‎<This message was edited>
[10/18/25, 5:20:11 PM] Venki Iyengar: Yes I carry lemon rice and curd rice and sandwiches and yes it weighs a little more but I think it really doesn’t matter that much
[10/18/25, 5:20:41 PM] Venki Iyengar: You don’t have to carry it on your person, there are too many bag options to put on your bike to carry these things ‎<This message was edited>
[10/18/25, 5:21:15 PM] Venki Iyengar: As for eating, we don’t really bother eating while riding. We stop and eat.
[10/18/25, 5:21:34 PM] Venki Iyengar: I do carry cut sandwiches in my to tube bag and can pop those into my mouth without stopping ‎<This message was edited>
[10/18/25, 5:21:43 PM] Venki Iyengar: But it’s minimal tbh
[10/18/25, 5:23:17 PM] Venki Iyengar: On our rando rides we usually stop at some cafe kind of place and pick up something.
[10/18/25, 5:24:15 PM] Venki Iyengar: The little break is actually very useful and good for the legs.
[10/18/25, 5:24:23 PM] Venki Iyengar: Can also stretch etc.
[10/18/25, 5:24:39 PM] Venki Iyengar: Just don’t let the break get too long
[10/18/25, 5:29:09 PM] Shriram: You just can’t rely on gels/bars/chews. Only way to get through anything 300+ is to take in real food that your palate likes
[10/18/25, 5:30:20 PM] Shriram: we have carried stuff like bakarwadis, chakali’s, dates.. etc, you name it we would have tried it among the riders at least once
[10/18/25, 5:31:01 PM] Shriram: I have poured a liter of Coca Cola into my bidon and used it once. Didn’t work well but couldn’t not try it out
[10/18/25, 5:34:03 PM] Arjun Periasamy: Amazing info all this helps 🙏🏽
[10/18/25, 5:36:07 PM] Venki Iyengar: And by and large everyone will agree that real food works best in the gut. Just a question of how much gels/bars will your gut tolerate and how much real food etc. can your afford (time/speed/weight) ‎<This message was edited>
[10/18/25, 5:38:47 PM] Venki Iyengar: And if it helps I have carried enough food for me for an entire 600k - that’s 35 ish hours x 170 (I’m on the lower extreme of cal consumption) = 6000 cals
[10/18/25, 5:39:14 PM] Venki Iyengar: What you cannot carry is water for the full ride so that’s what you really need to figure out how to get outside ‎<This message was edited>
[10/18/25, 5:50:08 PM] Mihir Sambhus: Ha ha. We should show the bags collection we have to carry all the food. For just in case situations I have always carried almost enough to last the ride and more often than not I come back with 60-70% of what I carry. After 2-3 water refills even the skratch superfuel or whatever other electrolyte or fuel we carry is too sweet to consume or too much for the gut. I switch to regular water. And everyone is so unique. This is one thing that you will learn what works in your case as you do more brevets

Recommendations for cold and rainy weather jackets?

[11/17/25, 7:50:14 PM] Nikhil Mahajan: Things to think about.. 
Not everything is expensive so, no need to rush for everything. But here's at least few categories that we will mostly consider.

1. Skull cap
2. Face mask/balaclava
2. Jacket 
3. Gloves
2. Hand warmers
3. Leg warmers
4. Socks
5. Shoe covers
6. Feet warmers

Some for cold weather and some rain proof as well. 

Lot of discussiona and suggestions are going to follow. ‎<This message was edited>

[11/18/25, 1:59:17 AM] Suryanarayanan Rajagopalan: Yes, generally:
Head gear = skull cap, ear muffs (for ears/forehead, something like https://a.co/d/3qHzRdI), neck gaiter
Body = base layer (regular cotton for normal rides, light thermal for colder rides, merino wool for coldest rides) + jersey + jacket
Legs = just leg cover
Feet = shoe cover
Hands = 2 pairs of gloves, third pair in bag
Helmet cover for rain ‎<This message was edited>

[12/2/25, 1:33:08 PM] Jagjot Kaur: Thanks for the suggestions guys 😄
I love you all. Thanks for trying to help. 

Few suggestions were based on some assumptions e.g. I don’t have a top tube bag. My bike is a small frame bike and a top tube bag was tried and discarded before the DR training season began.
I did most of my DR training with a handlebar Ornot bag and a saddle kit (that holds only flat repair stuff) while 
the Ornot handlebar bag was barely able to hold electrolytes, energy bars and did not have enough space for pickle juice mini bottles.

My summer jersey had bigger pockets to hold 2 sandwiches, 2 bananas, few stroopwaffles and a phone. 

During DR, I carried an additional power bank and putting that in my back pockets made me a literal mule. 

To add to this, winter Rapha jersey pockets are pretty useless capacity wise.

I wear 4 layers in winter and wouldn’t be surprised if I still feel cold. My cold and heat tolerance is very low and I am getting used to it while I attempt biking in such weather conditions

And I know we have men on the grp. I am unsure if Vijayshree ever talked about it but @⁨Bhupendra Jain⁩ did DR training with us, so he knows I am pretty open about most things. We are all sports people, so let me put this one out there. At times, women ride when on a period, so we have to carry some additional stuff 

I will digest all these good inputs and by Thursday evng, I will need to lean one way or the other. As you all say, Brevets are about figuring it out. 

Thanks for the suggestions.

But my original question- anyone has an action mount or solution for a light without consideration of options leading to domino effect 😁

[2/13/26, 4:20:29 PM] Venki Iyengar: Is that true? I do remember something about this
[2/13/26, 4:20:56 PM] Venki Iyengar: But anyway I think we will be well ahead of these times
[2/13/26, 4:23:04 PM] Siva Annamalai: Gemini says this :
Based on recent rule changes adopted by Audax Club Parisien (ACP) and followed by Randonneurs USA (RUSA), intermediate cut-off times are generally not enforced for standard brevets and populaires. 
Here are the key details:
Final Control Only: As of 2024, only the time at the final control is used to determine if a rider has successfully completed the brevet within the overall time limit.
Advisory Times: While intermediate checkpoints must still be visited to prove the route was followed, there are no enforced opening or closing times for them.
Guideline Use: Organizers may still provide intermediate opening/closing times, but these serve only as a guide to help riders manage their pace.
Exceptions:
LRM Events: For Grand Randonnées (1200 km or longer) sanctioned by Les Randonneurs Mondiaux (LRM), organizers may choose to enforce intermediate checkpoint times.
Unsupported/Extreme Conditions: If a rider misses an intermediate control or arrives exceptionally late, it may still raise issues regarding safety or validation. 
In summary, you cannot be disqualified for arriving "late" to an intermediate control on a standard RUSA brevet, provided you make the final checkpoint within the overall time limit.

[2/23/26, 12:12:33 AM] Suryanarayanan Rajagopalan: Trainer>>Starionary bike IMO, though I’m not an authority on this subject. A few points:
- ftp - Trainer goes with training apps (like Zwift, TrainerRoad, Rouvy etc) that understands your FTP and gives you workout plans (or you can tailor a custom plan or pick a workout randomly when you feel like it). The big issue is your FTP, though it looks like a standardized number, it feels like a number for a specific bike in a specific setup. So maybe you can figure out your ftp on stationary bike and program your workout on it, though I’m yet to see someone do it.
- ⁠mind factor - these training apps engage you more than a stationary bike and give more feedback (at least TrainerRoad), and that makes me feel engaged
- ⁠later, we may have group indoor biking sessions where we do group ride once or twice a week, and that’s based on Zwift (indoor trainer based)
- ⁠lastly, a bike fitter told me I need to train in the exact riding setup that I would do outdoors, that means similar bike with similar shoes with similar cleat positions. I guess a lot of it can be discounted but it does help me mentally, though I would assume any training is good. For indoor training, maybe you’ll use a different bike if you have 2 bikes but your shoes and riding positions maybe similar compared to stationary bike
[2/23/26, 12:14:22 AM] ~ Sunil: Fabulous
[2/23/26, 12:15:06 AM] Suryanarayanan Rajagopalan: Downsides of indoor riding:
- if you have only one bike, can be hassle to set it up for indoors while using it on weekend rides, need to watch for tire wear out (for on wheel trainers) or maybe have a different wheel
- ⁠training apps like TrainerRoad and Zwift cost 20-25$ per month
- ⁠need a place in your home for that trainer, which is @⁨Siva Annamalai⁩’s biggest concern
- ⁠buy a fan for cooling, a tv to watch while riding 😁

‎[2/25/26, 7:32:52 AM] Mihir Sambhus: Lot more enhancements yesterday. 
Couple of big feature drops: 
- Connect your strava account in my profile on top right. (you need to sign in first by connecting your gmail account) .. (you can keep your strava activities private if you wish). you will get a fitness score out of 100 based on your previous rides. 
- You can now add the rides you are going to from upcoming brevets page. after you login you can say going or in some cases you are not sure, so mark it as maybe. these rides will show up in your public profile page. 
- Another new feature one is Coaching *Advice* ! For the rides you have marked as going that show in your upcoming rides section, you will see an AI Advice button in that box. hover over to get some advice personalized to you based on your strava training data, previous brevets etc. 

Lot more features incoming. My garage. improved AI advice (with some personal preferences you enter in your profile page, weight, ftp, food preferences, etc etc.), customize your ride plan etc. ‎image omitted

[3/8/26, 1:49:17 PM] Jagjot Kaur: In 2020, I had written this poem on Women’s Day. Sharing it here for the one who like to read

 A Woman As I Am
 I get happy on the same thing many times,
I burst into laughter in minutes and cry in seconds,
What's minor for the world takes major space in my mind.

I don't like to be judged but don't restrict myself in the fear of being judged,
I get content if my today is a better version of my yesterday.

I feel others' pain and happiness; 
While empathy, gratitude, introspection constitute my persona,
Forgetfulness and forgiveness is a journey I'm uncovering and learning everyday.

I'm strong headed and opinionated,
Freedom for me is not only financial but emotional and mental.

I carry a saree in the Western world as comfortably as I carried shorts in the East,
I embrace my fragility and vulnerability as much as I embrace the confidence and poise.

I'm a woman who speaks with words, expresses with her eyes and laughs and cries with her heart
I'm a woman who prefers the ink flowing down her pen than alcohol down her throat to express bold emotions

I'm the light of the world as my parents named me. 
I'm Jagjot, I'm a Woman.

Happy Women's Day
